Non-commutative disintegrations: existence and uniqueness in finite dimensions
Arthur J. Parzygnat, Benjamin P. Russo

Abstract
Motivated by advances in categorical probability, we introduce non-commutative almost everywhere (a.e.) equivalence and disintegrations in the setting of C ∗ C^{*} -algebras. We show that C ∗ C^{*} -algebras (resp. W ∗ W^{*} -algebras) and a.e. equivalence classes of 2-positive (resp. positive) unital maps form a category. We prove non-commutative disintegrations are a.e. unique whenever they exist. We provide an explicit characterization for when disintegrations exist in the setting of finite-dimensional C ∗ C^{*} -algebras, and we give formulas for the associated disintegrations. † † 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 46L53 (Primary); 47B65, 81R15, 46M15 (Secondary). † † Key words and phrases. Bayesian inverse; categorical quantum mechanics; conditional expectation; optimal hypothesis; pre-Hilbert module; quantum measurement; quantum probability; regular conditional probability.



1
Introduction and outline

Regular conditional probabilities,
optimal hypotheses,
disintegrations of one measure over another consistent with a measure-preserving map,
conditional expectations,
perfect error-correcting codes, and
sufficient statistics
are all examples of a single mathematical notion. We call this notion a
disintegration
. Although we only make the connection between our definition of disintegration and the first three examples listed, relationships to the other notions are described in
[
35
]
, and further connections will be made in subsequent work.
In this paper, our primary focus is to provide necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence and uniqueness of disintegrations in the setting of finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras.

Developing this and related ideas is part of a larger program in extending Bayesian statistics to the
non-commutative
setting
[
31
,
35
,
36
]
in such a way so that it is compatible with a recently developed
categorical
framework for classical statistics
[
5
,
14
]
.
These recent advances in classical
categorical functional analysis and measure theory provide a suitable notion of disintegration
[
23
,
44
,
47
,
17
,
48
,
1
,
7
,
5
,
20
,
14
]
, whose diagrammatic formulation can be transferred from
a category of probability spaces to a category of states on
C^{*}
-algebras.
This is achieved by utilizing a fully faithful (contravariant) functor from the former to the latter
[
32
,
15
]
.
This categorical perspective offers a candidate for
generalizing disintegrations to non-commutative probability theory
without relying on the specific measure-theoretic details of classical probability theory.
Since a disintegration is a special kind of
Bayesian inverse

[
5
,
35
,
31
]
, this article serves as a step towards a theory of non-commutative Bayesian inversion.

Briefly, the definition of a disintegration
of a state
\omega
over another state
\xi
consistent with a
unital
∗
-homomorphism
F
preserving these states
is a completely positive unital map
R
in the
reverse
direction that is both state preserving and a left inverse of
F
modulo the null space of
\xi
.
If
∗
-homomorphisms are written as straight arrows
\to

and completely positive unital maps are written as squiggly arrows
\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!,

this definition of a disintegration can be summarized diagrammatically as

\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{R}

such that

\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{R}

and

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{B}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\xi}

(1.1)

in the category of finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras and completely positive unital maps. The right-most diagram commutes almost everywhere (a.e.), in a sense that we make precise in this article. We introduce and develop non-commutative a.e. equivalence in order to properly address the uniqueness properties of disintegrations.

The interpretation of completely positive unital maps as quantum conditional probabilities is not new
[
24
]
, but
we take this perspective further and include the relationships between states and partially reversible dynamics analogous to what
regular
conditional probabilities accomplish in classical statistics.
Our core result is Theorem
4.3
, which specializes to the case where
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are matrix algebras and
F
sends
B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
\mathrm{diag}(B,\dots,B)
.
If we express our states
\omega
and
\xi
in terms of density matrices
\rho
and
\sigma
, respectively, Theorem
4.3
says that a unique disintegration exists
if and only if there exists a density matrix
\tau
such that
\rho=\tau\otimes\sigma
. This is closely related to a well-known result on the existence of state-preserving conditional expectations
[
39
]
, but our notion generalizes it due to our weakened assumption of a.e. equivalence.

Our subsequent results are generalizations of this theorem and culminate in Theorem
5.108
, which assumes
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
are arbitrary finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras and
F
is an arbitrary unital
∗
-homomorphism.
We provide explicit formulas for disintegrations
and we analyze several examples, including one involving entanglement, which has its origins in the work of Einstein, Podolsky, and Rosen
[
10
]
.
In Example
5.99
,
we show how the standard classical theorem on the existence
and uniqueness of disintegrations (Theorem
5.1
) is a direct corollary of our theorem. We conclude by exploring consequences of our characterization theorem in the context of measurement in quantum information theory. Finally, Appendix
A
reviews stochastic maps (Markov kernels) and justifies our usage of the terminology ‘disintegration’ by showing that the diagrammatic notion agrees with a general measure-theoretic one.

2
Non-commutative a.e. equivalence

For classical probability spaces,
a.e. equivalence specifies the degree of uniqueness of disintegrations, Bayesian inverses, and conditional distributions.
The same is true in the quantum/non-commutative setting.
In this section, we first recall some relevant definitions involving states on
C^{*}
-algebras and completely positive maps from Paulsen
[
38
]
and Sakai
[
43
]
to establish notation and terminology. Afterwards, we define a.e. equivalence for linear maps between
C^{*}
-algebras in Definition
2.9
. We provide a more computationally useful definition for finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras in Lemma
2.26
.
In the rest of this section, we analyze several properties of a.e. equivalence.

Definition 2.1
.

A
C^{*}
-algebra
is an algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}

equipped with a unit
1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}},
an involution
{}^{*}:{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and a norm

\lVert\;\cdot\;\rVert:{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}

such that it is a
∗
-algebra,
it is closed with respect to the topology induced by its norm,
and it satisfies the
C^{*}
-identity,
which says
\lVert a^{*}a\rVert=\lVert a\rVert^{2}
for all
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Given a
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},

a
positive element
of
{{\mathcal{A}}}

is an element
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
for which there exists an
x\in{{\mathcal{A}}}

such that
a=x^{*}x.

The set of positive elements in
{{\mathcal{A}}}

is denoted by
{{\mathcal{A}}}^{+}.

Given another
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}},
a
positive map

\varphi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a linear map such that

\varphi({{\mathcal{B}}}^{+})\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}^{+}.

A linear map
\varphi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
is
unital
iff
\varphi(1_{{{\mathcal{B}}}})=1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}.

A
state
on a

C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a positive
linear unital functional
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}.

A
∗
-homomorphism

from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
to
{{\mathcal{B}}}
is a function
f:{{\mathcal{A}}}\to{{\mathcal{B}}}

preserving the
C^{*}
-algebra structure, namely
f
is linear,

f
is multiplicative
f(aa^{\prime})=f(a)f(a^{\prime}),

f
is unital
f(1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}})=1_{{{\mathcal{B}}}},

and
f(a^{*})=f(a)^{*}
for all
a,a^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

If
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
and
\xi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
are states, then a linear map

\varphi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
is said to be
state-preserving
whenever
\varphi\circ\omega=\xi
, and the notation
({{\mathcal{B}}},\xi)\mathrel{\hbox to15.92pt{\vbox to11.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.96034pt\lower-6.79025pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

All
C^{*}
-algebras and
∗
-homomorphisms will be unital unless specified otherwise.
Note that positive (and linear) maps on
C^{*}
-algebras are denoted with squiggly arrows

, while
∗
-homomorphisms are denoted with straight arrows
\to.

Example 2.2
.

For each
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}},
let
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
denote the set of

n\times n
complex matrices. The involution applied to
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

is given by
the conjugate transpose and is written as
A^{{\dagger}}
instead of

A^{*}
to be consistent with the standard notation used in quantum theory.
A
matrix algebra
is a
C^{*}
-algebra
of the form
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
for some
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.

If
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is another
C^{*}
-algebra, then

\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes{{\mathcal{A}}}\cong\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathcal{A}}})
,
the algebra of
n\times n

matrices with entries in
{{\mathcal{A}}},
admits a
C^{*}
-algebra structure
by matrix operations and a norm that can be obtained in many ways (cf. Chapter 1 in Paulsen
[
38
]
).

Our convention for the tensor product (also called the Kronecker product) of matrices will be

\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}&\cdots&a_{1m}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
a_{m1}&\cdots&a_{mm}\end{bmatrix}\otimes\begin{bmatrix}b_{11}&\cdots&b_{1n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
b_{n1}&\cdots&b_{nn}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}\!\begin{bmatrix}b_{11}&\cdots&b_{1n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
b_{n1}&\cdots&b_{nn}\end{bmatrix}&\cdots&a_{1m}\!\begin{bmatrix}b_{11}&\cdots&b_{1n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
b_{n1}&\cdots&b_{nn}\end{bmatrix}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
a_{m1}\!\begin{bmatrix}b_{11}&\cdots&b_{1n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
b_{n1}&\cdots&b_{nn}\end{bmatrix}&\cdots&a_{mm}\!\begin{bmatrix}b_{11}&\cdots&b_{1n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
b_{n1}&\cdots&b_{nn}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix},

(2.3)

which is induced by the isomorphism
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{mn}
determined by

\vec{e}_{1}\otimes\vec{e}_{1}\mapsto\vec{e}_{1},\quad\dots\quad\vec{e}_{1}\otimes\vec{e}_{n}\mapsto\vec{e}_{n},\quad\vec{e}_{2}\otimes\vec{e}_{1}\mapsto\vec{e}_{n+1},\quad\dots\quad\vec{e}_{m}\otimes\vec{e}_{n}\mapsto\vec{e}_{mn}.

(2.4)

Here,
\vec{e}_{i}
denotes the standard
i
-th unit vector in
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
regardless of
n
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be
C^{*}
-algebras.
Given
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}},

a linear map
\varphi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
is
n
-positive
iff

\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})}\otimes\varphi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes{{\mathcal{A}}}

is positive. The map

\varphi
is
completely positive
iff
\varphi
is
n
-positive for all
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.

A completely positive (unital) map will be abbreviated as
a CP (CPU) map.

The Choi–Kraus theorem gives a characterization of completely
positive maps between
matrix
algebras. This will be used often,
so we state it here to set notation
[
6
,
22
]
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Fix
n,m\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.
A linear map
R:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is completely positive
if and only if there exists a finite collection

\{R_{i}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\}
of linear maps such that

R=\sum_{i}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{i}}.

(2.7)

Here,
\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{i}}(A):=R_{i}AR_{i}^{{\dagger}}

for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).
The map
R
is CPU if and only if,
in addition,

\sum_{i}R_{i}R_{i}^{{\dagger}}=\mathds{1}_{m}.

A collection
\{R_{i}\}
satisfying (
2.7
)
is called a
Kraus decomposition
for
R.

Remark 2.8
.

The standard assumption in quantum information theory is to work with
completely positive
trace-preserving
maps instead of

unital
maps.
The former class, typically called
quantum operations/channels
(cf. Section 8.2 in Nielsen and Chuang
[
28
]
) is used in the Schrödinger representation when transforming physical states, while the latter is used in the Heisenberg representation when transforming physical observables.
We briefly explain the relationship between the two. A completely positive map

R:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is
unital if and only if the dual map

R^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

is trace-preserving. The dual map
R^{*}
is defined with respect
to the
Hilbert–Schmidt
(a.k.a.
Frobenius
)
inner product on a matrix algebra, which is given by

\langle A,B\rangle:={\rm tr}(A^{{\dagger}}B)

for all square matrices (of the same dimension)
A
and
B.

Therefore,
R^{*}
is the unique map satisfying

\langle R^{*}(A),B\rangle=\langle A,R(B)\rangle

for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})

and all
B\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

If
R=\sum_{i}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{i}}
is a Kraus decomposition of

R,
then
R^{*}=\sum_{i}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{i}^{{\dagger}}}
is a Kraus
decomposition of
R^{*}
because

\langle R^{*}(A),B\rangle=\langle A,R(B)\rangle=\sum_{i}{\rm tr}(A^{{\dagger}}R_{i}BR_{i}^{{\dagger}})=\sum_{i}{\rm tr}(R_{i}^{{\dagger}}A^{{\dagger}}R_{i}B)=\sum_{i}\langle R_{i}^{{\dagger}}AR_{i},B\rangle

by the cyclicity of the
trace. By a similar argument,

\langle R^{*}(\mathds{1}_{m}),B\rangle=\langle\mathds{1}_{m},R(B)\rangle={\rm tr}(R^{*}(B))

for all
B\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(B).

From this, it follows that

R
is unital if and only if
R^{*}
is trace-preserving.

In particular, if
\omega:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a state, then
its dual
\omega^{*}:{{\mathbb{C}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is determined by
the image
\omega^{*}(1)
of the unit
1
in
{{\mathbb{C}}}
, which is positive. Furthermore,
since
\omega
is unital,
\omega^{*}
is trace-preserving. Hence,

{\rm tr}(\omega^{*}(1))=1.
In other words,
\omega^{*}(1)
is a trace
1

positive matrix. This is called the
density matrix

associated to
\omega.

Finally,
\omega={\rm tr}(\omega^{*}(1)\;\cdot\;)
as states on
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

We now proceed to defining a.e. equivalence of linear maps on
C^{*}
-algebras.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be

C^{*}
-algebras,
let
F,F^{\prime}:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
be two
linear maps, and let
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a
state on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
(or more generally a positive linear functional). Let

\mathcal{N}_{\omega}:=\big\{a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\;:\;\omega(a^{*}a)=0\big\}

(2.10)

denote the
null space
of
\omega.

Since
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
is a left ideal of
{{\mathcal{A}}}

(see Construction 3.1 in
[
33
]
for details)
denote the quotient vector space by
{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}.

The maps
F
and
F^{\prime}
are said to be

equal almost everywhere (a.e.) with respect to
\omega

or
equal
\omega
-a.e.
iff the diagram (in the category of vector spaces and linear maps)

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}

(2.11)

commutes, i.e. iff
F(b)-F^{\prime}(b)\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
for all
b\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.

The map
{{\mathcal{A}}}\twoheadrightarrow{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
in
(
2.11
) is the quotient map of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
onto
{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}.

When
F
and
F^{\prime}
are equal
\omega
-a.e., the notation

F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\omega$}}{=}F^{\prime}

will be used.

The justification for the above terminology of a.e. equivalence is explained in the following illustrative example of finite probability spaces (cf. Appendix
A
for terminology).

Example 2.12
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}
be the commutative
C^{*}
-algebras of
complex-valued functions on the finite sets
X
and
Y,
respectively,
let
P:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state on
X
, and let
F,G:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
be two
positive unital maps. Then there exists a unique
probability measure
p
on
X
such that

\sum\limits_{x\in X}\varphi(x)p_{x}=P(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}
(see Section 2.6 of
[
32
]
for details).
Namely,

p_{x}:=P(e_{x}),

where
e_{x}
is the function on
X
defined by

X\ni x^{\prime}\mapsto e_{x}(x^{\prime}):=\delta_{xx^{\prime}}.

Similarly, there exist unique stochastic maps

f,g:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Y
such that

\big(F(\psi)\big)(x)=\sum_{y\in Y}\psi(y)f_{yx}\qquad\forall\;\psi\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y},\;\forall\;x\in X,

(2.13)

namely

X\ni x\mapsto\Big(Y\ni y\mapsto f_{yx}:=F(e_{y})(x)\Big)

(2.14)

and similarly for
G
with
g.

One can show that
the null space of
P
is given by

\mathcal{N}_{P}:=\big\{\varphi\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\;:\;P(\varphi^{*}\varphi)=0\big\}=\big\{\varphi\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}\;:\;\varphi\big|_{X\setminus N_{p}}=0\big\}=\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{x\in N_{p}}\big\{e_{x}\big\}\right),

(2.15)

where
N_{p}\subseteq X
is the measure-theoretic null space of
p

and
\varphi|_{X\setminus N_{p}}
denotes the restriction of
\varphi
to

X\setminus N_{p}.

Hence, the quotient
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}/\mathcal{N}_{P}
is isomorphic to functions on

X\setminus N_{p}
by the isomorphism

\begin{split}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}/\mathcal{N}_{P}&\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X\setminus N_{p}}\\
[\varphi]&\mapsto\varphi\big|_{X\setminus N_{p}}.\end{split}

(2.16)

As a result, the two positive unital maps
F,G:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{Y}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}^{X}

are equal
P
-a.e. if and only if
the associated stochastic maps

\tilde{f},\tilde{g}:X\setminus N_{p}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Y
defined by
the restrictions of
f
and
g
to
X\setminus N_{p},
respectively,
are equal.
This precisely means
f\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$p$}}{=}g.

We now proceed to establishing several important facts regarding non-commutative a.e. equivalence. First, if two maps are a.e. equivalent in terms of some state, then they pullback that state to the same state.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be
C^{*}
-algebras, let
\xi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state (or more generally a positive functional),
and let
\varphi,\psi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}

be linear maps.
If
\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\psi,

then
\xi\circ\varphi=\xi\circ\psi
.

Proof.

Let
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Then

\big|\xi\big(\varphi(a)-\psi(a)\big)\big|^{2}\leq\xi\Big(\big(\varphi(a)-\psi(a)\big)^{*}\big(\varphi(a)-\psi(a)\big)\Big)=0

(2.18)

by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for positive functionals (cf. Proposition 5.2.1 in Fillmore
[
11
]
) and because
\varphi(a)-\psi(a)\in\mathcal{N}_{\xi}.
Hence,

\xi\big(\varphi(a)\big)=\xi\big(\psi(a)\big).
Since
a
was arbitrary,

\xi\circ\varphi=\xi\circ\psi.

∎

The support of a state will also be useful in when formulating and proving our disintegration theorem.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state on a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
(or more generally a
W^{*}
-algebra). Then there exists a unique projection
P_{\omega}\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
(this means
P_{\omega}^{*}=P_{\omega}
and
P_{\omega}^{2}=P_{\omega}
) such that
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}={{\mathcal{A}}}(1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}-P_{\omega}).
Equivalently,
P_{\omega}
is characterized by

\omega(a)=\omega(aP_{\omega})=\omega(P_{\omega}a)=(\omega\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\omega}})(a)\qquad\forall\;a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

(2.20)

Proof.

See Section 1.14 of Sakai
[
43
]
.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

If
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is not a finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebra in Lemma
2.19
, then such a projection
P_{\omega}
for a state
\omega
need not exist. Indeed, if
{{\mathcal{A}}}=C(X),
continuous complex-valued functions on a connected compact Hausdorff space
X,
then there are no non-trivial projections and yet there are many states generating non-trivial null spaces. Such a projection does exist, however, if
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a
W^{*}
-algebra. Hence, several (but not all) of the results that follow involving such supports also hold for
W^{*}
-algebras.

Definition 2.22
.

Using the same notation from Lemma
2.19
,

P_{\omega}
is called the
support of
\omega
. Its complement
will be denoted by

P_{\omega}^{\perp}:=1_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}-P_{\omega}.

Example 2.23
.

When
{{\mathcal{A}}}=\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is a matrix algebra with a state

\omega:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}},
then

\omega={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)
for some unique density matrix
\rho\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

(cf. Remark
2.8
).
In this case,

P_{\omega}^{\perp}

is the projection onto the zero eigenspace of
\rho

and
P_{\omega}
satisfies
P_{\omega}\rho=\rho=\rho P_{\omega}.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
P
be a projection in a
C^{*}
-algebra (or
W^{*}
-algebra)
{{\mathcal{B}}}
and let
B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}.
Then
BP=0
implies

B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}P^{\perp}.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
every
B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
can be uniquely expressed as a sum of four terms

B=(P+P^{\perp})B(P+P^{\perp})=PBP+PBP^{\perp}+P^{\perp}BP+P^{\perp}BP^{\perp},

(2.25)

the non-zero ones of which are linearly independent.
∎

The decomposition (
2.25
) will be used frequently in this work,
1
1

1

The usage of such a decomposition is certainly not new. More recently, they have made an appearance in the study of Pierce and corner algebras, also in the context of conditional expectations
[
40
]
. We thank Chris Heunen for informing us of this reference.

particularly in conjunction with the support of a state. For example, we have the following alternative and computationally useful characterization of a.e. equivalence.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras, let
\xi:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state,
and let
\varphi,\psi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}

be
linear maps.
Let
P_{\xi}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
denote the support of
\xi.

i.

Then
\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\psi
if and only if
2
2

2

In the case where
{{\mathcal{A}}}=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}=\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, this says that the two maps
\varphi(A)
and
\psi(A)
agree when restricted to the subspace
P_{\xi}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\subseteq{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
.

\varphi(A)P_{\xi}=\psi(A)P_{\xi}
for all
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

ii.

If
\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\psi,

then

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ\varphi=\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ\psi.

Proof.

For the first claim, if
\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\psi
, then
\varphi(A)-\psi(A)=BP_{\xi}^{\perp}
for some
B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}
since
\mathcal{N}_{\xi}={{\mathcal{B}}}P_{\xi}^{\perp}
by Lemma
2.19
. Multiplying by
P_{\xi}
on the right gives item
i
. Conversely, if
\varphi(A)P_{\xi}=\psi(A)P_{\xi}
holds, then
\big(\varphi(A)-\psi(A)\big)P_{\xi}=0
.
Hence,
\varphi(A)-\psi(A)\in{{\mathcal{B}}}P_{\xi}^{\perp}
by Lemma
2.24
.
Item
ii
follows from item
i
by multiplying
\varphi(A)P_{\xi}=\psi(A)P_{\xi}
on the left by
P_{\xi}.

∎

Since CP maps between matrix algebras have particularly simple forms (cf. Theorem
2.6
),
it will also be useful to have a more quantitative version of Lemma
2.26
.
To state it, we first recall a general fact about the relationship
between two Kraus decompositions of a CP map of matrix algebras.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a CP map and suppose

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{i}}=\varphi=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{Ad}_{W_{j}}

(2.28)

are two Kraus decompositions of
\varphi
with
p\geq q.

Then there exists a
q\times p
matrix
U
that is a coisometry (meaning
UU^{{\dagger}}=\mathds{1}_{q}
, i.e. the rows of
U
are orthonormal) such that

V_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{q}u_{ji}W_{j}

for all
i\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}.

Here
u_{ji}
denotes the
ji
-th entry of
U.

Proof.

The reader is referred to Sections 6 and 7 of
[
34
]
for any unexplained details and terminology. First note that every such Kraus decomposition
\varphi=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{i}}
can be expressed as a Stinespring representation
\varphi=\pi\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{V},
where

\pi
and
V
are defined by

\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\ni A\xmapsto{\pi}\mathds{1}_{p}\otimes A\in\mathcal{M}_{p}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})

(2.29)

and

{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}^{pm}\xrightarrow{V:=[\;V_{1}\;\cdots\;V_{p}\;]}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n},

(2.30)

respectively (and similarly for
\varphi=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{Ad}_{W_{j}}
and
W:=\begin{bmatrix}W_{1}&\cdots&W_{q}\end{bmatrix}
). By the universal property of Stinespring representations (see Theorem 6.29 and the end of Section 7 in
[
34
]
), there exists a coisometry
3
3

3

Technically, the theorem referenced claims there exists a partial isometry. However, this partial isometry can be extended to a coisometry by similar techniques to those employed in Example 7.27 and Theorem 7.30 in
[
34
]
.

{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}\xrightarrow{U}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{q}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{q}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m}}
\scriptstyle{V}
\scriptstyle{W}
\scriptstyle{U\otimes\mathds{1}_{m}}

(2.31)

commutes. Writing

U\otimes\mathds{1}_{m}=\begin{bmatrix}u_{11}\mathds{1}_{m}&\cdots&u_{1p}\mathds{1}_{m}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
u_{q1}\mathds{1}_{m}&\cdots&u_{qp}\mathds{1}_{m}\\
\end{bmatrix},

(2.32)

we see that commutativity of (
2.31
) gives

\begin{bmatrix}V_{1}&\cdots&V_{p}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}W_{1}&\cdots&W_{q}\end{bmatrix}(U\otimes\mathds{1}_{n}),

(2.33)

which is the result claimed.
∎

Lemma 2.34
.

Fix a positive integer
n,
let
\xi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state, and
let
\varphi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a CPU map such that

\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})}.

Let
P_{\xi}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
denote the support of
\xi
and
let
\varphi
have a Kraus decomposition
\varphi=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{i}}.

Then there exist complex numbers
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
such that

P_{\xi}V_{i}=\alpha_{i}P_{\xi}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,p\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}=1.

(2.35)

Proof.

Since
\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})},
Lemma
2.26
implies

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}\circ\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}V_{i}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi}}.

(2.36)

Furthermore, Lemma
2.27
implies there exists a
1\times p
coisometry

U
such that

P_{\xi}V_{i}=u_{1i}P_{\xi},
where
u_{1i}
is the
1i
-th entry
of
U.

Set
\alpha_{i}:=u_{1i}.

Since
U
is a coisometry,
the first (and only) column of
U
is a unit vector, i.e.
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}|u_{1i}|^{2}=1,

which proves the claim.
∎

Remark 2.37
.

Using the notation of Lemma
2.34
,

P_{\xi}V_{i}=\alpha_{i}P_{\xi}
says

V_{i}=\alpha_{i}P_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}V_{i}P_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}V_{i}P_{\xi}^{\perp}.

(2.38)

If we choose a basis in which the density matrix
\xi^{*}(1)
is diagonal with its non-zero eigenvalues all appearing on the top left, then (
2.38
) reads

V_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{i}\mathds{1}_{r}&0\\
V_{i}^{\mathrm{bl}}&V_{i}^{\mathrm{br}}\end{bmatrix},

(2.39)

where
r
is the rank of
\xi^{*}(1),
and where
V_{i}^{\mathrm{bl}}
is an

(n-r)\times r
matrix while
V_{i}^{\mathrm{br}}
is an
(n-r)\times(n-r)
matrix.

It turns out that Lemma
2.34

holds even when the support
P_{\xi}
from the equations is removed (cf. Theorem
2.48
below). To prove this, it seems convenient to recall the notion of a pre-Hilbert
C^{*}
-algebra module due to Paschke
[
37
]
.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a (unital)
C^{*}
-algebra. A
pre-Hilbert
{{\mathcal{A}}}
-module
is a
left
4
4

4

Paschke defines a
right
module structure instead of a left one. This does change some properties, but we have modified them appropriately. One such property is the Paschke–Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
in (
2.44
).

{{\mathcal{A}}}
-module
\mathcal{E}
together with a linear-conjugate linear map
5
5

5

This means
\langle\!\langle s+t,u\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle s,u\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle t,u\rangle\!\rangle

for all
s,t,u\in\mathcal{E}.
The other properties usually associated with sesqui-linearity (with conjugate linearity in the second coordinate) follow from the other conditions in the definition since the algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is unital.

\langle\!\langle\;\cdot\;,\;\cdot\;\rangle\!\rangle:\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}
satisfying the following properties

i.

\langle\!\langle s,s\rangle\!\rangle\geq 0
for all
s\in\mathcal{E},

ii.

\langle\!\langle s,t\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle t,s\rangle\!\rangle^{*}
for all
s,t\in\mathcal{E},

iii.

\langle\!\langle as,t\rangle\!\rangle=a\langle\!\langle s,t\rangle\!\rangle
for all
s,t\in\mathcal{E}
and
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}},
and

iv.

\langle\!\langle s,s\rangle\!\rangle=0
if and only if
s=0
(this is called
non-degeneracy
of
\langle\!\langle\;\cdot\;,\;\cdot\;\rangle\!\rangle
).

\langle\!\langle\;\cdot\;,\;\cdot\;\rangle\!\rangle
is called the

{{\mathcal{A}}}
-valued inner product
on
\mathcal{E}.

Remark 2.41
.

It follows from this definition that
\langle\!\langle s,at\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle s,t\rangle\!\rangle a^{*}
for all

s,t\in\mathcal{E}
and
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Lemma 2.42
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and
\mathcal{E}
a pre-Hilbert module over
{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Then

\mathcal{E}\ni s\mapsto\lVert s\rVert_{\mathcal{E}}:=\sqrt{\lVert\langle\!\langle s,s\rangle\!\rangle\rVert}

(2.43)

defines a norm on
\mathcal{E}.
Furthermore,

\langle\!\langle t,s\rangle\!\rangle\langle\!\langle s,t\rangle\!\rangle\leq\lVert t\rVert_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}\langle\!\langle s,s\rangle\!\rangle

(2.44)

for all
s,t\in\mathcal{E}.

Proof.

See Proposition 2.3 in Paschke
[
37
]
or Section 3.14 in Fillmore
[
11
]
.
∎

Example 2.45
.

Fix
n,m,p\in{{\mathbb{N}}}.
Let
{}_{m}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
denote the vector space
of
m\times n
complex matrices. Set
{{\mathcal{A}}}:=\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and

\mathcal{E}:={}_{m}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})^{p},
the vector space direct sum of
p
copies of
{}_{m}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
. Denote elements of

\mathcal{E}
by
\vec{A}:=(A_{1},\dots,A_{p})
so that

A_{i}\in{}_{m}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}.

Define the
left
{{\mathcal{A}}}
-module structure on
\mathcal{E}
to be

B\vec{A}:=(BA_{1},\dots,BA_{p})
for all
\vec{A}\in\mathcal{E}
and
B\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Define the
{{\mathcal{A}}}
-valued inner product by

\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}\ni(\vec{A},\vec{B})\mapsto\left\langle\!\!\left\langle\vec{A},\vec{B}\right\rangle\!\!\right\rangle:=\sum_{i=1}^{p}A_{i}B_{i}^{{\dagger}}.

(2.46)

Straightforward matrix algebra shows
\mathcal{E}

is indeed a pre-Hilbert
{{\mathcal{A}}}
-module
with these structures.
In fact,
\mathcal{E}
is also a
right

\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-module
satisfying

\left\langle\!\!\left\langle\vec{A}C,\vec{B}\right\rangle\!\!\right\rangle=\left\langle\!\!\left\langle\vec{A},\vec{B}C^{{\dagger}}\right\rangle\!\!\right\rangle\qquad\forall\;\vec{A},\vec{B}\in\mathcal{E}\text{ and }C\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(2.47)

However,
\mathcal{E}
is
not
a (right) pre-Hilbert module with
respect to this action.

Theorem 2.48
.

Fix
n\in{{\mathbb{N}}},

let
\xi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state, and
let
\varphi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a CPU map such that

\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})}.

Let
\varphi
have a Kraus decomposition
\varphi=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{i}}.

Then there exist complex numbers
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
such that

V_{i}=\alpha_{i}\mathds{1}_{n}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,p\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}=1.

(2.49)

In particular,
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})}.

Proof.

Let
P_{\xi}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
denote the support of
\xi
.
In order to proceed avoiding as many indices and sums as possible,
we will first introduce
a certain pre-Hilbert
C^{*}
-algebra module based on the number
p
of Kraus operators assumed for
\varphi.

Set
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})^{p}
and equip this
with the pre-Hilbert
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-module structure
from Example
2.45
.
By Remark
2.37
,

\vec{V}=P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}+\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}+\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},

(2.50)

where

\vec{\alpha}:=\big(\alpha_{1}\mathds{1}_{n},\dots,\alpha_{p}\mathds{1}_{n}\big),\quad\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}:=P_{\xi}^{\perp}\vec{V}P_{\xi},\quad\text{ and }\quad\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}:=P_{\xi}^{\perp}\vec{V}P_{\xi}^{\perp}.

(2.51)

Note that
C\vec{\alpha}=\vec{\alpha}C
for all
C\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

The identities

\langle\!\langle\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle=\mathds{1}_{n},\qquad\langle\!\langle P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle={0},\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle={0}

(2.52)

follow directly from the definitions.
The fact that
\varphi
is unital means
\mathds{1}_{n}=\sum\limits_{i}V_{i}V_{i}^{{\dagger}}.
In terms of the
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-valued inner product,
this becomes

\begin{split}\mathds{1}_{n}&=\langle\!\langle\vec{V},\vec{V}\rangle\!\rangle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vigeneralvector})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\langle\!\langle P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}+\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}+\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}+\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}+\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vibasicidentities})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\underbrace{\langle\!\langle P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha},P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle}_{P_{\xi}}+\langle\!\langle P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle.\end{split}

(2.53)

Since

P_{\xi}\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}=\vec{0}\quad\text{ and }\quad P_{\xi}\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}=\vec{0},

(2.54)

it follows that

\langle\!\langle\vec{X},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle P_{\xi}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\langle\!\langle\vec{X},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle P_{\xi}=0\qquad\forall\;\vec{X}\in\mathcal{E}

(2.55)

by the properties of the pre-Hilbert module structure
(see Remark
2.41
). Hence, multiplying
(
2.53
) by
P_{\xi}
on the right and simplifying
gives

0=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle

and similarly
\langle\!\langle P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle=0.

Furthermore,
\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},P_{\xi}^{\perp}\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle=0
follows immediately from the definition of
\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}
. Putting these two together gives

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle={0}.

(2.56)

Hence, the unitality of
R
condition (
2.53
) simplifies to

P_{\xi}^{\perp}=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle.

(2.57)

Now, write
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
as (cf. Equation (
2.25
))

A=P_{\xi}AP_{\xi}+P_{\xi}AP_{\xi}^{\perp}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}^{\perp}

(2.58)

in terms of the support
P_{\xi}
of
\xi
and its orthogonal complement
P_{\xi}^{\perp}
.
Using this decomposition,

\begin{split}\varphi(A)P_{\xi}&=\sum_{i=1}^{p}V_{i}AV_{i}^{{\dagger}}P_{\xi}=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}A,\vec{V}\rangle\!\rangle P_{\xi}\overset{\text{Rmk~\ref{rmk:paschkeswap}}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\langle\!\langle\vec{V}A,P_{\xi}\vec{V}\rangle\!\rangle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vigeneralvector})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\langle\!\langle\vec{V}A,P_{\xi}\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Paschkematrixinnerproduct})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\langle\!\langle\vec{V},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle AP_{\xi}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vibasicidentities}) \& (\ref{eq:Viblalphaiszero})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\left(P_{\xi}+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle\right)AP_{\xi}\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:idecomposeA})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\left(P_{\xi}+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle\right)\Big(P_{\xi}AP_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}\Big)\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:iPkillsVblandVbr})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}P_{\xi}AP_{\xi}+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}\end{split}

(2.59)

for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).
But since
\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})},
this equals

P_{\xi}AP_{\xi}+P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}
by Lemma
2.26
item
i
.
Identifying terms,
\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}=P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}
for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}),
i.e.
\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle
acts as the identity on
n\times n
matrices
of the form
P_{\xi}^{\perp}AP_{\xi}.
This combined with the fact that

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle=P_{\xi}^{\perp}\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle P_{\xi}^{\perp}

implies

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle=P_{\xi}^{\perp}.

(2.60)

This implies

\langle\!\langle\vec{\alpha},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle^{*}\langle\!\langle\vec{\alpha},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle=P_{\xi}^{\perp}.

Hence, by the Paschke–Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (Lemma
2.42
),

P_{\xi}^{\perp}\leq\lVert\vec{\alpha}\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vibasicidentities})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle.

(2.61)

On the other hand, (
2.57
) entails

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle\leq P_{\xi}^{\perp}

by condition
i
in Definition
2.40
.
These two inequalities force

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle=P_{\xi}^{\perp}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle=0.

(2.62)

By non-degeneracy of the
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-valued inner product
on
\mathcal{E},
this forces
\vec{V}^{\mathrm{bl}}=\vec{0},
i.e.

P_{\xi}V_{i}P_{\xi}^{\perp}=0\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,p\}.

(2.63)

Finally, using these relations and the properties of the
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-valued inner product,

\langle\!\langle P_{\xi}^{\perp}\vec{\alpha}-\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},P_{\xi}^{\perp}\vec{\alpha}-\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vibasicidentities})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}P_{\xi}^{\perp}-\langle\!\langle\vec{\alpha},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle-\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Vibralphaissupportcomp}) \& (\ref{eq:iVbrVbrVblVbl})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}0,

(2.64)

which, by non-degeneracy of the
\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-valued inner product,
proves

\vec{V}^{\mathrm{br}}=P_{\xi}^{\perp}\vec{\alpha},

(2.65)

i.e.

P_{\xi}^{\perp}V_{i}P_{\xi}^{\perp}=\alpha_{i}P_{\xi}^{\perp}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,p\}.

(2.66)

Putting this all together gives

\vec{V}=\vec{\alpha},\quad\text{i.e.}\quad V_{i}=\alpha_{i}\mathds{1}_{n}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,p\}.

(2.67)

The fact that
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
follows
immediately from this result.
∎

Remark 2.68
.

It should be stressed how surprising Theorem
2.48
is.
Even if
\xi
is a pure state, so that its support is a rank one projection, a.e. equivalence of a CPU map to the identity is strong enough to enforce
equality
of that CPU map to the identity, regardless of how large the dimension of the Hilbert space is. We feel this gives us a precise sense of how “probability zero” objects (the projection onto the orthogonal complement of the support of
\xi
in this case) in quantum theory cannot be disregarded in the way that they can be in classical probability theory
[
19
]
.

Remark 2.69
.

Theorem
2.48
might seem to suggest that
if
\xi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a state and
if
\varphi,\psi:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})

are two CPU maps, then

\varphi\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\psi

implies
\varphi=\psi.
The following example shows this is false in general.
Let
s\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}
and let
\chi:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be the
map that takes the trace of the bottom right part of a matrix, namely

\chi(A):=\sum_{i=s+1}^{m}a_{ii},

(2.70)

where
a_{ii}
is the
ii
-th entry of
A.

Note that this map is positive and therefore CP since the codomain of
\chi
is
{{\mathbb{C}}}
(cf. Theorem 3 in Stinespring
[
45
]
). Note, however, that
\chi
is not unital. Similarly, the trace map
{\rm tr}:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
is CP (but not unital).
Now, consider the following two maps

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})&\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\\
A&\xmapsto{\,\;\varphi\;}\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(A)\mathds{1}_{n}\quad\text{ and}\\
A&\xmapsto{\,\;\psi\;}\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(A)P_{\xi}+\frac{1}{m-s}\chi(A)P_{\xi}^{\perp}.\\
\end{split}

(2.71)

Note that
\varphi
and
\psi
are not equal.
Nevertheless,
\varphi
and
\psi
are
\xi
-a.e. equivalent because
\varphi(A)P_{\xi}=\psi(A)P_{\xi}
for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}}).
A simple calculation shows
\psi
and
\varphi
are unital. Furthermore, they are both completely positive as their
p
-ampliations are

\varphi_{p}(\;\cdot\;)=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}_{p}(\;\cdot\;)\otimes\mathds{1}_{n}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\psi_{p}(\;\cdot\;)=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}_{p}(\;\cdot\;)\otimes P_{\xi}+\frac{1}{m-s}\chi_{p}(\;\cdot\;)\otimes P_{\xi}^{\perp},

(2.72)

respectively.
Here,
{\rm tr}_{p}
and
\chi_{p}
are the
p
-ampliations of
{\rm tr}
and
\chi,
which are positive.

Remark 2.73
.

The conclusion of Theorem
2.48
is false if

\varphi
is assumed to only be CP but not unital.
A simple counter-example is the CP map

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})&\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\\
A&\xmapsto{\,\;\varphi\;}A+{\rm tr}(A)P_{\xi}^{\perp}.\end{split}

(2.74)

Here,
\varphi
is
\xi
-a.e. equivalent to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}(C)}
but is not equal to it.

Remark 2.75
.

Using the same notation and assumptions as in Theorem
2.48
, if
\varphi
is
\xi
-a.e. equivalent to a
∗
-isomorphism, then it equals that
∗
-isomorphism. However, if
\xi:\mathcal{M}_{mp}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a state and
\varphi:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{mp}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is a CPU map that is
\xi
-a.e. equivalent to a
∗
-homomorphism, then it is not necessarily equal to that
∗
-homomorphism (unless
p=1
). A simple counter-example is
\varphi(B):=\mathrm{diag}(B,{\rm tr}(B)\mathds{1}_{m},\dots,{\rm tr}(B)\mathds{1}_{m})
and
\xi
the state represented by the density matrix
\frac{1}{m}\mathrm{diag}(\mathds{1}_{m},0,\dots,0)
. Then
\varphi
is
\xi
-a.e. equivalent to the
∗
-homomorphism
B\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(B,\dots,B)
, but it is not equal to it (unless
m=1
or
p=1
).

The following corollary of Theorem
2.48
is similar to a fact used frequently
in the area of reversible quantum operations (cf. the proof of Theorem 2.1 in Nayak and Sen
[
27
]
).

Corollary 2.76
.

Let
F:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and
R:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be CPU maps with
Kraus decompositions

R=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{i}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{Ad}_{F_{j}}.

(2.77)

If
R\circ F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
for some state
\xi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
,
then there exist complex numbers
\{\alpha_{ij}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,\dots,p\}\\
j\in\{1,\dots,q\}\end{subarray}}
such that

R_{i}F_{j}=\alpha_{ij}\mathds{1}_{m}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i,j}|\alpha_{ij}|^{2}=1.

(2.78)

In particular,
R\circ F=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})}.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
2.48
.
∎

3
Categories of C*-algebras, states, and morphisms

We prove that
non-commutative probability spaces
,

C^{*}
-algebras equipped with states, and a.e. equivalence classes of
CPU maps
(in fact, 2-positive unital maps) form a category. In fact, finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras and a.e. equivalence classes of positive unital maps form a category.
The following Cauchy–Schwarz type inequality, due to Kadison
[
21
]
,
for positive unital and 2-positive unital maps
is useful in proving many of these claims.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}
be
C^{*}
-algebras and let
\varphi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}
be
a positive unital map.

i.

If
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
is self-adjoint, then

\varphi(a)^{2}\leq\varphi(a^{2}).

ii.

If
\varphi
is 2-positive, then

\varphi(a)^{*}\varphi(a)\leq\varphi(a^{*}a)
for all
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Proof.

See Theorem 1.3.1 and Corollary 1.3.2 in
[
46
]
and Proposition 3.3 in
[
38
]
.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C},{{\mathcal{B}}}
, and
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be
C^{*}
-algebras, let
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state on
{{\mathcal{A}}},

and let
G,G^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}
and
F,F^{\prime}:{{\mathcal{B}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
be 2-positive (or Schwarz-positive) unital maps. If
F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\omega$}}{=}F^{\prime}

and

G\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}G^{\prime}
, where
\xi:=\omega\circ F,

then

F\circ G\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\omega$}}{=}F^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime}.

Proof.

By assumption, the diagrams

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{G^{\prime}}

and

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}

(3.3)

both commute.
For the composite, we have

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\xi}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}

(3.4)

The left part of this diagram commutes
by commutativity of the left diagram in
(
3.3
).
It would be convenient to have a function
