\mathcal{R}_{(P_{\xi})_{k}}(B_{k}):=B_{k}(P_{\xi})_{k}
for all

B_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{n_{k}}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

Proof.

The condition
R\circ F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{B}}
holds if and only if (cf. Lemma
2.26
)

\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi}}\circ R\circ F=\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi}},

which holds if and only if

\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi}}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}=\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi}}\circ\iota_{j}

for all
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}.

Finally, this is equivalent to

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 114.20026pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-107.77838pt\raise 21.12012pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.65001pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi}}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 36.13148pt\raise 21.12012pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.65001pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi}}\circ\iota_{j}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-114.20026pt\raise-21.12012pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.49722pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{(P_{\xi})_{k}}\circ\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 29.7096pt\raise-21.12012pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.49722pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{(P_{\xi})_{k}}\circ\pi_{k}\circ\iota_{j}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\qquad\forall\;j,k\in\{1,\dots,t\},

(5.37)

which is equivalent to the claim (
5.36
) because

\pi_{k}\circ\iota_{j}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_{n_{k}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}&\mbox{ if $k=j$}\\
0&\mbox{ if $k\neq j$}\\
\end{cases}.\qed

(5.38)

It may be helpful to visualize the map
\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}\equiv(R\circ F)_{kj}

as the following composite of CP (not necessarily unital) maps

\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{m_{1}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{m_{s}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{n_{1}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{n_{t}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{n_{1}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{n_{t}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{n_{k}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{j}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}

(5.39)

The following is an analogue of Lemma
2.34

to direct sums of matrix algebras.

Lemma 5.40
.

Using the same notation
as in Lemma
5.35
and assuming
R\circ F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{B}}}},

write

R=\begin{pmatrix}R_{11}&\cdots&R_{1s}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
R_{t1}&\cdots&R_{ts}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad F=\begin{pmatrix}F_{11}&\cdots&F_{1t}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
F_{s1}&\cdots&F_{st}\end{pmatrix}

(5.41)

where the completely positive (not necessarily unital) maps

R_{ki}:\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n_{k}}({{\mathbb{C}}})

and
F_{ij}:\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\to\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}})

have Kraus decompositions,
12
12

12

We will see in the text surrounding (
5.54
)
that there exists a Kraus decomposition of

F_{ij}
such that the index
\gamma_{ij}
runs from
1
to
c_{ij}
instead of

m_{i}n_{j}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{t}c_{ik}n_{k}n_{j}.

This just means that
F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}
is zero
when
\gamma_{ij}
exceeds
c_{ij}.

R_{ki}=\sum_{\beta_{ki}=1}^{n_{k}m_{i}}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad F_{ij}=\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{m_{i}n_{j}}\mathrm{Ad}_{F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}}

(5.42)

with
R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m_{i}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n_{k}}
and

F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n_{j}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m_{i}}
linear maps.

i.

For each
k\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q},

there exist
a collection of complex numbers

\{\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},\gamma_{ik}}\},
indexed by
\gamma_{ik}\in\{1,\dots,c_{ik}\},\beta_{ki}\in\{1,\dots,n_{k}m_{i}\},i\in\{1,\dots,s\},

such that

P_{\xi_{k}}R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}F_{ik;\gamma_{ik}}=\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},\gamma_{ik}}P_{\xi_{k}}

(5.43)

for all
\beta_{ki}\in\{1,\dots,n_{k}m_{i}\},\;\gamma_{ik}\in\{1,\dots,n_{k}m_{i}\},\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}

and

\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\beta_{ki}=1}^{n_{k}m_{i}}\sum_{\gamma_{ik}=1}^{m_{i}n_{k}}|\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},\gamma_{ik}}|^{2}=1.

(5.44)

ii.

For every pair
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}
and
k\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q}

with
j\neq k
,

P_{\xi_{k}}R_{ki,\beta_{ki}}F_{ij,\gamma_{ij}}=0

(5.45)

for all
\beta_{ki}\in\{1,\dots,n_{k}m_{i}\},\;\gamma_{ij}\in\{1,\dots,n_{j}m_{i}\},\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

Proof.

Computing
\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}
for
j,k\in\{1,\dots,t\}
gives

\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}R_{ki}\circ F_{ij}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\beta_{ki}=1}^{n_{k}m_{i}}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{m_{i}n_{j}}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}}.

(5.46)

Suppose
q_{k}>0.
Then, (
5.36
) becomes

\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi_{k}}}\circ\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}_{P_{\xi_{k}}}&\mbox{ if }k=j\\
0&\mbox{ if }k\neq j\end{cases}.

(5.47)

i.

In the case
k=j,
(
5.47
) entails

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi_{k}}}\circ\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{k}=\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi_{k}}}

(5.48)

upon multiplying by
P_{\xi_{k}}
on the left.
Combining this with (
5.46
) and
Lemma
2.27
(by following a similar proof to that of Lemma
2.34
),
there exist complex numbers

\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},\gamma_{ik}}

satisfying (
5.43
) and (
5.44
).

ii.

In the case
k\neq j,
(
5.47
) becomes

\mathrm{Ad}_{P_{\xi_{k}}}\circ\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}=0

(5.49)

upon multiplying by
P_{\xi_{k}}
on the left.
This implies

P_{\xi_{k}}R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}=0

(5.50)

by (
5.46
) and
Lemma
2.27
.
∎

At this point, it is helpful to make the conclusions of
Lemma
5.40
even more explicit
by further explicating
P_{\xi_{k}}R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}.

The Kraus operator
R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m_{i}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n_{k}}
can be partitioned into
block sums of matrices based on the multiplicity of
F
in the following way

R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}=\big[\overbrace{\underbrace{V_{ki;\beta_{ki};11}}_{n_{k}\times n_{1}\text{ matrix}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{V_{ki;\beta_{ki};1c_{i1}}}_{n_{k}\times n_{1}\text{ matrix}}}^{n_{k}\times(c_{i1}n_{1})\text{ matrix}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\overbrace{\underbrace{V_{ki;\beta_{ki};t1}}_{n_{k}\times n_{t}\text{ matrix}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{V_{ki;\beta_{ki};tc_{it}}}_{n_{k}\times n_{t}\text{ matrix}}}^{n_{k}\times(c_{it}n_{t})\text{ matrix}}\big]

(5.51)

due to (
5.13
).
Based on this partitioning, the unitality condition on
R
reads

\mathds{1}_{n_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\beta_{ki}=1}^{m_{i}n_{k}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}V_{ki;\beta_{ki};j\gamma_{ij}}V_{ki;\beta_{ki};j\gamma_{ij}}^{{\dagger}}\qquad\forall\;k\in\{1,\dots,t\}.

(5.52)

due to (
5.18
).
Furthermore, the definition of
F
from (
5.12
)
says

F_{ij}(B_{j})=\mathrm{diag}(0,\dots,0,\dots,B_{j},\dots,B_{j},\dots,0,\dots,0)\qquad\forall\;B_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(5.53)

This implies that the (adjoint of the) Kraus operators

F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n_{j}}\to{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m_{i}}
of

F_{ij}:\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\to\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}})

have the following partitioned form

F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}^{{\dagger}}=\big[\overbrace{\underbrace{0}_{n_{j}\times n_{1}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{0}_{n_{j}\times n_{1}}}^{n_{j}\times(c_{i1}n_{1})}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\overbrace{\underbrace{0}_{n_{j}\times n_{j}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{0}_{n_{j}\times n_{j}}}^{n_{j}\times(c_{ij}n_{j})}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\overbrace{\underbrace{0}_{n_{j}\times n_{t}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{0}_{n_{j}\times n_{t}}}^{n_{j}\times(c_{it}n_{t})}\big],

(5.54)

where the identity matrix
\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}
is in the
\gamma_{ij}
-th
n_{j}\times n_{j}
subblock
inside the
n_{j}\times(c_{ij}n_{j})
block indicated (and all other entries are
0
).
In particular, the index
\gamma_{ij}
runs from
1
to
c_{ij}
(as opposed to
m_{i}n_{j}
).
Therefore, the product
R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}
is

R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}F_{ij;\gamma_{ij}}=V_{ki;\beta_{ki};j\gamma_{ij}},

(5.55)

which is an
n_{k}\times n_{j}
matrix. The following result is a generalization of Equation (
4.9
) to the direct sum case.

Lemma 5.56
.

Under the same assumptions as in
Lemma
5.40
,
for every
{k\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q}}
, there exist
a collection of complex numbers
\{\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},\gamma_{ik}}\},
indexed by
\gamma_{ik}\in\{1,\dots,c_{ik}\},

\beta_{ki}\in\{1,\dots,n_{k}m_{i}\},
and
i\in\{1,\dots,s\},

such that

R_{ki;\beta_{ki}}=\big[\overbrace{\underbrace{0}_{n_{k}\times n_{1}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{0}_{n_{k}\times n_{1}}}^{n_{k}\times(c_{i1}n_{1})}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\overbrace{\underbrace{\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},1}\mathds{1}_{n_{k}}}_{n_{k}\times n_{k}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},c_{ik}}\mathds{1}_{n_{k}}}_{n_{k}\times n_{k}}}^{n_{k}\times(c_{ik}n_{k})}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\overbrace{\underbrace{0}_{n_{k}\times n_{t}}\;\;\;\cdots\;\;\;\underbrace{0}_{n_{k}\times n_{t}}}^{n_{k}\times(c_{it}n_{t})}\big],

(5.57)

for all
\beta_{ki}\in\{1,\dots,n_{k}m_{i}\},i\in\{1,\dots,s\}

and

\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\beta_{ki}=1}^{n_{k}m_{i}}\sum_{\gamma_{ik}=1}^{c_{ik}}|\alpha_{k;i,\beta_{ki},\gamma_{ik}}|^{2}=1.

(5.58)

Proof.

In analogy to the proof of Theorem
2.48
,
for every
k\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q}
,
let
\mathcal{E}_{k}

be the pre-Hilbert
\mathcal{M}_{n_{k}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-module
consisting of vectors of
n_{k}\times n_{k}
matrices whose vector
components are labelled by
the triple of indices
\gamma_{ik},\beta_{ki},i.

Let
\vec{V}_{k}
be the vector whose vector components are the
n_{k}\times n_{k}
matrices
V_{ki;\beta_{ki};k\gamma_{ik}}.

The first case of
Lemma
5.40
implies
there exists a vector
\vec{\alpha}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
whose vector
components are
constant multiples of the identity matrix satisfying

\vec{V}_{k}=P_{\xi_{k}}\vec{\alpha}_{k}+\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}}+\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}},

(5.59)

where

\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}}:=P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{V}_{k}P_{\xi_{k}},\qquad\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}:=P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{V}_{k}P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\langle\!\langle\vec{\alpha}_{k},\vec{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle=\mathds{1}_{n_{k}}.

(5.60)

Similarly, for every pair
(k,j)\in\{1,\dots,t\}\times\{1,\dots,t\}
such that
j\neq k

and such that
q_{k}>0,
let
\mathcal{E}_{kj}

be the pre-Hilbert
\mathcal{M}_{n_{k}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
-module
consisting of vectors of
n_{k}\times n_{j}
matrices
whose vector components are labelled by
the triple of indices
\gamma_{ij},\beta_{ki},i.

Let
\vec{V}_{kj}
be the vector of the
n_{k}\times n_{j}
matrices
whose components are given by
V_{ki;\beta_{ki};j\gamma_{ij}}.

The second case of
Lemma
5.40
implies

\vec{V}_{kj}=\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{bl}}+\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{br}},

where

\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{bl}}:=P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{V}_{kj}P_{\xi_{j}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{br}}:=P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{V}_{kj}P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}.

(5.61)

The equalities

\langle\!\langle P_{\xi_{k}}\vec{\alpha}_{k},\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle=0

(5.62)

all follow immediately from the definitions.
Unitality of
R
takes on the form

\mathds{1}_{n_{k}}=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k},\vec{V}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=1,j\neq k}^{t}\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{kj},\vec{V}_{kj}\rangle\!\rangle

(5.63)

by (
5.52
).
By expanding out (
5.63
) and multiplying
on the right by
P_{\xi_{k}},

completely similar arguments to those in the proof of Theorem
2.48
, specifically the discussion surrounding
Equations (
2.53
) through (
2.56
),
prove

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle={0}.

Hence, the unitality condition (
5.63
) simplifies to

P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle+\sum_{j=1,j\neq k}^{t}\left(\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{bl}},\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{bl}}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle\right)

(5.64)

analogously to (
2.53
).
Now, computing
\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j}
in terms of the
pre-Hilbert module inner product gives

(\pi_{k}\circ R\circ F\circ\iota_{j})(A_{j})=\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{kj}A_{j},\vec{V}_{kj}\rangle\!\rangle\qquad\forall\;A_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})

(5.65)

for all
k,j
(when
j=k,
remove one of the indices from
\vec{V}_{kk}
)
by (
5.55
). When
j=k,
multiplying this equation on the right by
P_{\xi_{k}}
(which equals
(P_{\xi})_{k}
since
q_{k}>0
) and combining this with
Lemma
5.35
gives

\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{\alpha}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle=P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}

by following an argument exactly analogous to (
2.59
) and
the text surrounding this equation.
Similarly, combining this
result with the Paschke–Cauchy–Schwarz
inequality gives

P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\leq\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle.

On the other hand, (
5.64
) says

P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\geq\langle\!\langle\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}},\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}\rangle\!\rangle.

Therefore, following analogous lines of thought to those
from (
2.62
)
to (
2.65
)
gives

\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{br}}=P_{\xi_{k}}^{\perp}\vec{\alpha}_{k},\qquad\vec{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{bl}}=\vec{0},\qquad\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{bl}}=\vec{0},\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{V}_{kj}^{\mathrm{br}}=\vec{0}.

(5.66)

Therefore,

\vec{V}_{k}=\vec{\alpha}_{k}
and
\vec{V}_{kj}=\vec{0}
.
Expanding out the vector entries coming from the definitions of

\mathcal{E}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{kj}
completes the proof.
∎

Given a state-preserving
∗
-homomorphism
({{\mathcal{B}}},\xi)\xrightarrow{F}({{\mathcal{A}}},\omega)
, it may be useful to know
how the density matrices associated to
\xi
and
\omega

are related. The following fact describes this relationship.
It is a generalization of the “tracing out degrees of freedom” method
in quantum theory.

Proposition 5.67
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},F,\omega,
and
\xi
be as in
Notation
5.7
, and let

\omega\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}{\rm tr}(\rho_{i}\;\cdot\;)\quad\text{ and }\quad\xi\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{t}q_{j}{\rm tr}(\sigma_{j}\;\cdot\;)

(5.68)

be decompositions of the states
\omega
and
\xi
as described in
Lemma
5.27
. Then the following facts hold.

i.

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,s\},
there exists a
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}

such that
c_{ij}>0.

ii.

If there exists a
j\in\{1,\dots,s\}
such that
c_{ij}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\},

then
q_{j}=0.

iii.

Finally,

q_{j}\sigma_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}p_{i}\rho_{i;jj;\gamma_{ij}\gamma_{ij}}\qquad\forall\;j\in\{1,\dots,t\},

(5.69)

where
\rho_{i;jj;\gamma_{ij}\gamma_{ij}}
is the
n_{j}\times n_{j}
matrix obtained from
\rho_{i}
in the following way.
Since
m_{i}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{t}c_{ik}n_{k},
each

m_{i}\times m_{i}
matrix
\rho_{i}
has
a block matrix decomposition

\rho_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{i;11}&\cdots&\rho_{i;1t}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\rho_{i;t1}&\cdots&\rho_{i;tt}\end{bmatrix},

(5.70)

where
\rho_{i;jk}
is a
(c_{ij}n_{j})\times(c_{ik}n_{k})
matrix.
This matrix further breaks up into subblocks

\rho_{i;jk}=\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{i;jk;11}&\cdots&\rho_{i;jk;1c_{ik}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\rho_{i;jk;c_{ij}1}&\cdots&\rho_{i;jk;c_{ij}c_{ik}}\end{bmatrix},

(5.71)

where
\rho_{i;jk;\gamma_{ij}\gamma_{ik}}
is an
n_{j}\times n_{k}
matrix.

Remark 5.72
.

The contrapositive of part
ii
of Proposition
5.67

will be used occasionally in certain technical points later. It states
that if
q_{j}>0,
there exists at least one
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}
such that

c_{ij}>0.

In other words,
F
is injective almost everywhere. This should be compared to Lemma
5.4
.
Furthermore, using partial traces,
Equation (
5.69
) becomes

q_{j}\sigma_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}{\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}(\rho_{i;jj}),

(5.73)

where
\rho_{i}
is decomposed as in (
5.70
).

Proof of Proposition
5.67
.

you found me!

i.

Since
m_{i}>0
and
m_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{t}c_{ij}n_{j},

there must exist a non-zero
c_{ij}
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}.

ii.

Suppose there exists a
j\in\{1,\dots,s\}
such that
c_{ij}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

Then
F_{ij}(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}})=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

Since
\omega\circ F=\xi,
this shows
\xi(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}})=0.

But
\xi(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}})=q_{j}{\rm tr}(\sigma_{j})=q_{j}
so that
q_{j}=0.

iii.

This follows from taking the adjoint of the equation
\omega\circ F=\xi,

which gives

F^{*}\circ\omega^{*}=\xi^{*}\implies F^{*}\big(\omega^{*}(1)\big)=\xi^{*}(1).

(5.74)

Expanding out these expressions and extracting the
j
-th term gives

q_{j}\sigma_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}F_{ij}^{*}(\rho_{i})\qquad\forall\;j\in\{1,\dots,t\}.

(5.75)

Applying (
5.42
) and (
5.54
) gives the desired result. ∎

A consequence of Lemma
5.56
is the following
fact regarding the existence and
uniqueness of disintegrations on finite-dimensional

C^{*}
-algebras.
It is a generalization of Theorem
4.3

to direct sums of matrix algebras and is the main theorem of the present work.

Theorem 5.76
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},F,\omega,
and
\xi
be as in
Notation
5.7
and Proposition
5.67
.

i.

A disintegration
R
of
\omega
over
\xi
consistent with
F

exists if and only if
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}
there exist
non-negative matrices
\tau_{ji}\in\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
such that
13
13

13

{N}_{q}
was introduced in
Notation
5.30
.

{\rm tr}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\tau_{ji}\right)=1\qquad\forall\;j\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus{N}_{q}

(5.77)

and

p_{i}\rho_{i}=\mathrm{diag}(q_{1}\tau_{1i}\otimes\sigma_{1},\dots,q_{t}\tau_{ti}\otimes\sigma_{t})\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

(5.78)

ii.

Furthermore, if
R^{\prime}
is another disintegration
of
\omega
over
\xi
consistent with
F,
then

R^{\prime}\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}R

and

R^{\prime}_{ji}=R_{ji}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}\qquad\forall\;j\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus{N}_{q}.

(5.79)

iii.

Finally, if such a disintegration
R
exists, a formula for the
ji
-th component of the disintegration is given by

R_{ji}(A_{i})={\rm tr}_{\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})}\big((\tau_{ji}\otimes\mathds{1}_{n_{j}})A_{i;jj}\big)

(5.80)

for all
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q}
and for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.
Here,
A_{i;jj}
is uniquely defined by the decomposition
as a

t\times t
matrix

A_{i}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}A_{i;11}&\cdots&A_{i;1t}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
A_{i;t1}&\cdots&A_{i;tt}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(5.81)

where the
kl
-th subblock,
A_{i;kl},
is a
(c_{ik}n_{k})\times(c_{il}n_{l})
matrix.

Proof.

Proving the first item will provide proofs of the subsequent claims.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose a disintegration
R
exists. The condition
\xi\circ R=\omega
is equivalent to
R^{*}\big(\xi^{*}(1)\big)=\omega^{*}(1)

by Lemma
5.21
. Hence, using the notation
from (
5.68
), this equation gives

R^{*}\big(\xi^{*}(1)\big)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}R_{11}^{*}&\cdots&R_{t1}^{*}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
R_{1s}^{*}&\cdots&R_{ts}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}\sigma_{1}\\
\vdots\\
q_{t}\sigma_{t}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\rho_{1}\\
\vdots\\
p_{s}\rho_{s}\\
\end{pmatrix}\equiv\omega^{*}(1),

(5.82)

which is equivalent to

p_{i}\rho_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}q_{j}R_{ji}^{*}(\sigma_{j})\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

(5.83)

To compute
R_{ji}^{*}(\sigma_{j}),

we can follow an analogous computation
to that from (
4.11
). First, when
q_{j}>0,
we obtain

\begin{split}R_{ji}^{*}(\sigma_{j})&=\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\mathrm{Ad}_{R_{ji;\beta_{ji}}^{{\dagger}}}(\sigma_{j})\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:PRds})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\overline{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}}\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}}\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\sigma_{j}\begin{bmatrix}0&{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}}\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}&\cdots&{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}}\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}&0\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

(5.84)

where the top
0
block in the left matrix is a

\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{j-1}c_{ik}n_{k}\right)\times n_{j}
matrix
and the bottom
0
block in the left matrix is a

\bigg(\sum\limits_{k=j+1}^{t}c_{ik}n_{k}\bigg)\times n_{j}
matrix.
Keeping track of these sizes, we obtain

R_{ji}^{*}(\sigma_{j})=\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&|\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}|^{2}\sigma_{j}&\cdots&\overline{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}}\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}\sigma_{j}&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\overline{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}}\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}\sigma_{j}&\cdots&|\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}|^{2}\sigma_{j}&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix},

(5.85)

where the top-left
0
matrix is a
\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{j-1}c_{ik}n_{k}\right)\times\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{j-1}c_{ik}n_{k}\right)
matrix and
the bottom-right
0
matrix is a
\bigg(\sum\limits_{k=j+1}^{t}c_{ik}n_{k}\bigg)\times\bigg(\sum\limits_{k=j+1}^{t}c_{ik}n_{k}\bigg)
matrix.
Define the
c_{ij}\times c_{ij}
matrix
\tau_{ji}
to be

\tau_{ji}:=\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}|\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}|^{2}&\cdots&\overline{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}}\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\overline{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}}\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},1}&\cdots&|\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},c_{ij}}|^{2}\end{bmatrix}

(5.86)

so that the
\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}
-th entry of
\tau_{ji}
is given by

\tau_{ji;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}=\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\overline{\alpha_{j;i;\beta_{ji},\gamma_{ij}}}\alpha_{j;i;\beta_{ji},\eta_{ij}}.

(5.87)

Notice that
\tau_{ji}
is defined only when
c_{ij}>0
and when
q_{j}>0.

Furthermore, when it is defined,
\tau_{ji}
is a non-negative matrix and

\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\rm tr}(\tau_{ji})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}|\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},\gamma_{ij}}|^{2}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:sumalphaisidentity})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}1,

(5.88)

which shows
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{s}\tau_{ji}
is a density matrix
(again, when
q_{j}>0
).
The sum in (
5.88
) is guaranteed to have at least one term
due to
Remark
5.72
.
Second, when
q_{j}=0,
then
q_{j}R_{ji}^{*}(\sigma_{j})=0
so that this term does not
contribute to the sum in (
5.83
).
Therefore, in this case,

\tau_{ji}
can be chosen to be an arbitrary non-negative matrix
provided that
c_{ij}>0.
If
c_{ij}=0,
then
\tau_{ji}
does not exist
and any expression involving such a
\tau_{ji}
should be excluded.
Then,

p_{i}\rho_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}q_{j}R_{ji}^{*}(\sigma_{j})\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Rjipullsbackstate})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\begin{bmatrix}q_{1}\tau_{1i}\otimes\sigma_{1}&&0\\
&\ddots&\\
0&&q_{t}\tau_{ti}\otimes\sigma_{t}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

(5.89)

Note that this sum after the first equality is not empty by part
i
of Proposition
5.67
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
For the converse, suppose the non-negative matrices

\tau_{ji}\in\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})

satisfying (
5.77
) and
(
5.78
) exist.
Denote the
\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}
-th entry of
\tau_{ji}
by
\tau_{ji;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}.

For each pair of indices
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,t\},

define
R^{\prime}_{ji}:\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
in the
following way. Write an element
A_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
as in (
5.81
).
Then, write
A_{i;kl}
as a
c_{ik}\times c_{il}
matrix consisting of

n_{k}\times n_{l}
matrices
indexed as in
A_{i;kl;\gamma_{ik}\eta_{il}}.
Set
14
14

14

Note that the swapping of the
\gamma_{ij}
and
\eta_{ij}
indices
in Equation (
5.90
) is not a typo. In addition, note that if
q_{j}>0
and
c_{ij}=0,
then the sum in the top case is empty and gives, by definition of an empty sum,
0.

R_{ji}^{\prime}(A_{i}):=\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{\gamma_{ij},\eta_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\tau_{ji;\eta_{ij}\gamma_{ij}}A_{i;jj;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}&\mbox{ if $q_{j}>0$}\\
\frac{1}{sm_{i}}{\rm tr}(A_{i})\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}&\mbox{ if $q_{j}=0$ }\\
\end{cases}.

(5.90)

A direct calculation shows that this formula equals (
5.80
) when
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q}
.
Set
R^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!\mathcal{B}
to be the
t\times s
matrix of linear maps whose

ji
-th entry is
R_{ji}^{\prime}
from (
5.90
). Then
R^{\prime}
is a disintegration of
\omega
over
\xi
consistent with
F.

The proof of this is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.3

though one must keep track of indices more carefully.
Unitality of
R^{\prime}
follows from

\sum_{i=1}^{s}R^{\prime}_{ji}(\mathds{1}_{m_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\gamma_{ij},\eta_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\tau_{ji;\eta_{ji}\gamma_{ij}}\mathds{1}_{m_{i};jj;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\tau_{ji;\gamma_{ji}\gamma_{ij}}\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\rm tr}(\tau_{ji})\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}

(5.91)

whenever
q_{j}>0

because
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{s}{\rm tr}(\tau_{ji})=1.

When
q_{j}=0,
one obtains

\sum_{i=1}^{s}R^{\prime}_{ji}(\mathds{1}_{m_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\frac{1}{sm_{i}}{\rm tr}(\mathds{1}_{m_{i}})\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}.

(5.92)

We will now show

\pi_{j}\circ R^{\prime}\circ F=\pi_{j}
for all
j
satisfying
q_{j}>0.

First, note that

(R^{\prime}\circ F)(\vec{B})=\begin{pmatrix}R^{\prime}_{11}&\cdots&R^{\prime}_{1s}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
R^{\prime}_{t1}&\cdots&R^{\prime}_{ts}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{diag}(\overbrace{B_{1},\dots,B_{1}}^{c_{11}\text{ times}},\dots,\overbrace{B_{t},\dots,B_{t}}^{c_{1t}\text{ times}})\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{diag}(\underbrace{B_{1},\dots,B_{1}}_{c_{s1}\text{ times}},\dots,\underbrace{B_{t},\dots,B_{t}}_{c_{st}\text{ times}})\end{pmatrix}.

(5.93)

Focusing on the
j
-th term when
q_{j}>0
, one obtains

\sum_{i=1}^{s}R^{\prime}_{ji}\Big(\mathrm{diag}(\underbrace{B_{1},\dots,B_{1}}_{c_{i1}\text{ times}},\dots,\underbrace{B_{t},\dots,B_{t}}_{c_{it}\text{ times}})\Big)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\tau_{ji;\gamma_{ji}\gamma_{ij}}B_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\rm tr}(\tau_{ji})B_{j}=B_{j}.

(5.94)

Although the equality
\pi_{j}\circ R^{\prime}\circ F=\pi_{j}
fails when
q_{j}=0,
the equation
R^{\prime}\circ F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{B}}}}

still holds.
Furthermore,
R^{\prime}
is state-preserving because

\begin{split}\omega\big(\vec{A}\big)&=\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\rm tr}(p_{i}\rho_{i}A_{i})\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:separabilitydisrectsumcase})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{t}q_{j}{\rm tr}\big((\tau_{ji}\otimes\sigma_{j})A_{i;jj}\big)\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
q_{j}>0\end{subarray}}^{t}q_{j}{\rm tr}\left(\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1,\eta_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\tau_{ji;\eta_{ij}\gamma_{ij}}\sigma_{j}A_{i;jj;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}\right)\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:canonicaldisintegerationds})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
q_{j}>0\end{subarray}}^{t}q_{j}{\rm tr}\big(\sigma_{j}R^{\prime}_{ji}(A_{i})\big)=(\xi\circ R^{\prime})\big(\vec{A}\big)\end{split}

(5.95)

for all
\vec{A}\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.
To show
R^{\prime}
is CP,
it suffices to show each
R^{\prime}_{ji}
is CP by Lemma
5.15
.
This follows from the equality between the formulas (
5.80
) and (
5.90
) when
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus N_{q}
. The case when
j\in N_{q}
gives a CP map as well since the trace in (
5.90
) is a CP map.

Finally, we prove the uniqueness condition (
5.79
) for disintegrations.
The condition
R^{\prime}\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\xi$}}{=}R

is equivalent to

\sum_{i=1}^{s}R^{\prime}_{ji}(A_{i})(P_{\xi})_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}R_{ji}(A_{i})(P_{\xi})_{j}\qquad\forall\;\vec{A}\in{{\mathcal{A}}},\;j\in\{1,\dots,t\}.

(5.96)

When
q_{j}=0,
this equality holds trivially because

(P_{\xi})_{j}=0.

When
q_{j}\neq 0,
Lemma
5.56

guarantees the existence of complex
numbers
\{\alpha_{j;i,\beta_{ji},\gamma_{ij}}\}
and Kraus operators

\{R_{ji;\beta_{ji}}\}
for
R_{ji}

satisfying the conditions in the statement of that theorem.
Therefore, by carefully working out the matrix operations,
one obtains

\begin{split}R_{ji}(A_{i})&\overset{\text{Lemma~\ref{thm:formofdisintoncalg}}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\beta_{ji}=1}^{n_{j}m_{i}}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\sum_{\eta_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\alpha_{j;i;\beta_{ji},\gamma_{ij}}\overline{\alpha_{j;i;\beta_{ji},\eta_{ij}}}A_{i;jj;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:entriesoftau})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\gamma_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\sum_{\eta_{ij}=1}^{c_{ij}}\tau_{ji;\eta_{ij}\gamma_{ij}}A_{i;jj;\gamma_{ij}\eta_{ij}}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:canonicaldisintegerationds})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}R_{ji}^{\prime}(A_{i})\qquad\forall\;A_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}}).\end{split}

(5.97)

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 5.98
.

By applying the trace to both sides of (
5.78
), one obtains

p_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{t}q_{j}{\rm tr}(\tau_{ji})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

An immediate corollary of Theorem
5.76

is the standard existence and uniqueness theorem of
regular conditional probabilities from classical finite probability.
We work this out in full detail as an example.

Example 5.99
.

Using the notation from Theorem
5.76
,
suppose
m_{i}=1
and
n_{j}=1
for all
i,j.

Then,
\rho_{i}=1=\sigma_{j}
for all
i,j.

Furthermore, since each
m_{i}=1,
the multiplicity
is drastically restricted since
m_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{t}c_{ij}n_{j}.

By this equality, for each
i,
there exists a unique
j
such that

c_{ij}=1
and all other
c_{ik}=0.
In other words,
there exists a unique function
f:\{1,\dots,s\}\to\{1,\dots,t\}

such that

c_{ij}=\delta_{f(i)j}\equiv\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }f(i)=j\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

(5.100)

This implies

F=\begin{bmatrix}\delta_{f(1)1}&\cdots&\delta_{f(1)t}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\delta_{f(s)1}&\cdots&\delta_{f(s)t}\\
\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad F^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}\delta_{f(1)1}&\cdots&\delta_{f(s)1}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\delta_{f(1)t}&\cdots&\delta_{f(s)t}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

(5.101)

Hence,

q_{j}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:qjsigmaj})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}F^{*}_{ij}(1)\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Fcommutativecase})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}\delta_{f(i)j}=\sum_{i\in f^{-1}(j)}p_{i},

(5.102)

which reproduces the probability-preserving condition
(X,p)\xrightarrow{f}(Y,q)
, assuming

X=\{1,\dots,s\}
and
Y=\{1,\dots,t\}.

In what follows, we will construct, without any additional
assumptions, non-negative matrices

\tau_{ji}\in\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
satisfying
(
5.77
) and (
5.78
)
as well as a disintegration

R=\begin{bmatrix}r_{11}&\cdots&r_{1s}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
r_{t1}&\cdots&r_{ts}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

(5.103)

This will prove that a disintegration automatically exists in this case.
First note that if
j\neq f(i),
the set
\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is just a singleton
so that we only have a chance of constructing
\tau_{ji}
when
j=f(i).

In this case,
c_{if(i)}=1
and such a matrix will be a
1\times 1

matrix, i.e. a non-negative number.
We set

\tau_{ji}:=\begin{cases}p_{i}/q_{j}&\mbox{ if $q_{j}>0$ and $j=f(i)$}\\
\#&\mbox{ if $q_{j}=0$ and $j=f(i)$}\\
\text{DNE}&\mbox{ if $c_{ij}=0$}\\
\end{cases},

(5.104)

where
\#
can be chosen to be any non-negative number.
Note that if there exists a
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}
for which

c_{ij}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,s\},
then
q_{j}=0

by part
ii
of Proposition
5.67
.
For such
j,

\tau_{ij}
cannot be defined for any
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

Nevertheless,

{\rm tr}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\tau_{ji}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\tau_{ji}=\sum_{i\in f^{-1}(j)}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{j}}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:commutativestatepreserving})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}1\qquad\forall\;j\in\{1,\dots,s\}\setminus{N}_{q}

(5.105)

proves (
5.77
).
Secondly, because there exists a unique
j
for each
i
such that
c_{ij}=1,

\begin{split}\mathrm{diag}(q_{1}\tau_{1i}\otimes\sigma_{1},\dots,q_{t}\tau_{ti}\otimes\sigma_{t})&=q_{f(i)}\tau_{f(i)i}=q_{f(i)}\begin{cases}p_{i}/q_{f(i)}&\mbox{ if $q_{f(i)}>0$}\\
\#&\mbox{ if $q_{f(i)}=0$}\\
\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}p_{i}&\mbox{ if $q_{f(i)}>0$}\\
0&\mbox{ if $q_{f(i)}=0$}\\
\end{cases}\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.\end{split}

(5.106)

Note that if
q_{f(i)}=0,
then
p_{i}=0
by (
5.102
). Hence, this proves
(
5.78
).
Although this already proves a disintegration exists via Theorem
5.76
, it is
fruitful to construct it based on the proof of
Theorem
5.76
and compare it to the classical
disintegration from Theorem
5.1
.
Using the construction of a disintegration from
(
5.90
), we get

\begin{split}r_{ji}&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:canonicaldisintegerationds})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\begin{cases}\tau_{ji}&\mbox{ if $q_{j}>0$ and $c_{ij}=1$}\\
0&\mbox{ if $q_{j}>0$ and $c_{ij}=0$}\\
1/s&\mbox{ if $q_{j}=0$}\\
\end{cases}\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:taujicommutativecase})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\begin{cases}p_{i}/q_{j}&\mbox{ if $q_{j}>0$ and $j=f(i)$}\\
0&\mbox{ if $q_{j}>0$ and $j\neq f(i)$}\\
1/s&\mbox{ if $q_{j}=0$}\\
\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}p_{i}\delta_{f(i)j}/q_{j}&\mbox{ if $q_{i}>0$}\\
1/s&\mbox{ if $q_{j}=0$}\end{cases}.\end{split}

(5.107)

This reproduces formula (
5.2
) for an ordinary disintegration.

Finally, we end this section with a generalization of Theorems
5.76
and
4.30

by allowing for arbitrary (unital)
∗
-homomorphisms
F:{{\mathcal{B}}}\to{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Theorem 5.108
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},F,\omega,
and
\xi
be as in
Notation
5.7

and Proposition
5.67
except that
F
is now
an arbitrary (unital)
∗
-homomorphism, but not necessarily of the form (
5.12
). Then, a disintegration
R
exists
if and only if
there exist unitary matrices
U_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{m_{i}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
and
non-negative matrices
\tau_{ji}\in\mathcal{M}_{c_{ij}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
such that

\mathrm{Ad}_{\vec{U}}\circ F
is of the form (
5.12
),

{\rm tr}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\tau_{ji}\right)=1\qquad\forall\;j\in\{1,\dots,t\}\setminus{N}_{q}

(5.109)

and

p_{i}U_{i}^{{\dagger}}\rho_{i}U_{i}=\mathrm{diag}(q_{1}\tau_{1i}\otimes\sigma_{1},\dots,q_{t}\tau_{ti}\otimes\sigma_{t})\qquad\forall\;i\in\{1,\dots,s\}.

(5.110)

Furthermore, any two such disintegrations are unique
\xi
-a.e.

Proof.

This follows from an argument analogous to the proof of Theorem
4.30
.
∎

6
Example: measurement in quantum mechanics

It is instructive to work out the following example due to its connection with measurement in quantum mechanics (it may be helpful at this point to review Example
2.12
for notation).
We will also avoid using the results of Theorem
5.76
and will instead provide a self-contained analysis since this is simple enough in this special case.
Fix
m\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
, let
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a self-adjoint matrix with spectrum
\sigma(A)\subseteq{{\mathbb{R}}}
, and let
\omega={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;):\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state. The matrix
A
induces the
∗
-homomorphism uniquely determined by

\begin{split}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}&\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\\
e_{\lambda}&\mapsto P_{\lambda},\end{split}

(6.1)

where
P_{\lambda}
is the orthogonal projection onto the
\lambda
eigenspace associated to
A.

This pulls back the state
\omega
to a probability measure
q
on
\sigma(A)
whose evaluation on
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
will be denoted by
q_{\lambda}.
The pullback state will be denoted by
\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle
, where
\langle\;\cdot\;,\;\cdot\;\rangle
is the natural inner product on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}
induced by the basis
\{e_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}.
Physically, the number
q_{\lambda}
is interpreted as the probability that the state
\omega
takes the value
\lambda
when the observable
A
is measured.
If a disintegration
R:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}
exists, it is uniquely determined by the collection of PU maps
R_{\lambda}
defined by

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})&\mathrel{\hbox to16.72pt{\vbox to11.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.36052pt\lower-6.99443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.80775pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.74 -6.56 L 9.91 -6.56 C 8.87 -6.56 8.36 -7.75 7.32 -7.75 C 6.58 -7.75 5.93 -7.17 5.26 -6.56 C 4.58 -5.96 3.94 -5.38 3.19 -5.38 C 2.44 -5.38 1.8 -5.96 1.12 -6.56 C 0.45 -7.17 -0.2 -7.75 -0.94 -7.75 C -1.69 -7.75 -2.34 -7.17 -3.01 -6.56 C -3.68 -5.96 -4.33 -5.38 -5.08 -5.38 L -11.29 -6.56 L -11.29 -6.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.96053pt}{-4.74443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{\lambda}}{{\mathbb{C}}}\\
A&\xmapsto{\hskip 33.2897ptR_{\lambda}\hskip 33.2897pt}\langle e_{\lambda},R(A)\rangle\end{split}

(6.2)

and indexed by
\lambda\in\sigma(A).
Because these are states on
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, they uniquely determine a density matrix
\rho_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
, i.e.

R_{\lambda}={\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda}\;\cdot\;),\quad\rho_{\lambda}\geq 0,\quad{\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda})=1,\quad\text{ and }\quad R=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}\mathrm{ev}_{\lambda}^{*}\circ R_{\lambda}.

(6.3)

Because
R
must be state-preserving to be a disintegration, this entails

{\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;)=\langle{q},R(\;\cdot\;)\rangle\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Rpropertiesmeasurement})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}q_{\lambda}{\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda}\;\cdot\;)\;\;\implies\;\;\rho=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}q_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}.

(6.4)

The other condition for
R
to be a disintegration is

R\circ F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle$}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}}
,
which says

\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}b_{\lambda}e_{\lambda}-R\left(F\left(\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}b_{\lambda}e_{\lambda}\right)\right)\in\mathcal{N}_{\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle}\qquad\forall\;\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}b_{\lambda}e_{\lambda}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}.

(6.5)

Expanding this out, relabeling indices, and using part
i
of Lemma
2.26
gives

\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}b_{\lambda}e_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}\left(\sum_{\mu\in\sigma(A)}b_{\mu}{\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu})\right)e_{\lambda}.

(6.6)

Linear independence of the
e_{\lambda}
then gives the constraints

b_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\in\sigma(A)}b_{\mu}{\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu})\qquad\forall\;\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}.

(6.7)

Since the
b
’s can be chosen arbitrarily and independently (indeed, set
b_{\mu}:=\delta_{\mu\nu}
for various
\nu
), we conclude

{\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu})=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\qquad\forall\;\mu,\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}.

(6.8)

Since
\rho_{\lambda}
is a positive matrix,

{\rm tr}(\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu})={\rm tr}(P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu})=0
if and only if
P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu}=0
whenever
\mu\neq\lambda.

In what follows, we will prove
\rho_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}.
To see this, first let
\vec{u}\in\mathrm{Im}(P_{\mu})
and
\vec{v}\in\mathrm{Im}(P_{\nu})
, where
\mu,\nu\in\sigma(A)\setminus\{\lambda\}
. Then
P_{\mu},P_{\nu}\leq P_{\lambda}^{\perp}
and

\begin{split}0&\leq\langle\vec{u}+\vec{v},P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}(\vec{u}+\vec{v})\rangle\\
&=\langle\vec{u},P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\vec{u}\rangle+\langle\vec{u},P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\vec{v}\rangle+\langle\vec{v},P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\vec{u}\rangle+\langle\vec{v},P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\vec{v}\rangle\\
&=\langle\vec{u},P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu}\vec{u}\rangle+\langle\vec{u},P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\nu}\vec{v}\rangle+\langle\vec{v},P_{\nu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu}\vec{u}\rangle+\langle\vec{v},P_{\nu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\nu}\vec{v}\rangle\\
&=2{{\mathfrak{Re}}}\langle\vec{u},P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\nu}\vec{v}\rangle,\end{split}

(6.9)

where we have freely used the facts
P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\vec{u}=P_{\mu}\vec{u}=\vec{u}
and
P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\vec{v}=P_{\nu}\vec{v}=\vec{v}
together with the self-adjointness and orthogonality of these projections. Since
\vec{u}
and
\vec{v}
can be arbitrary, positivity of
P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}
guarantees that
P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\nu}=0
for all
\mu,\nu\in\sigma(A)\setminus\{\lambda\}.

So far, we have shown
\rho_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}+P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}+P_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{\perp}.

What is left to show is that
P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}=0
for all
\mu\in\sigma(A)\setminus\{\lambda\}
(which would imply
P_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\mu}=0
by taking the adjoint).
Now, let
\vec{u}\in\mathrm{Im}(P_{\mu})
and
\vec{v}\in\mathrm{Im}(P_{\lambda})
, where
\mu\in\sigma(A)\setminus\{\lambda\}
. Positivity of
\rho_{\lambda}
gives

0\leq\langle\vec{u}+\vec{v},\rho_{\lambda}(\vec{u}+\vec{v})\rangle=2{{\mathfrak{Re}}}\langle\vec{u},P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}\vec{v}\rangle+\langle\vec{v},\rho_{\lambda}\vec{v}\rangle

(6.10)

by a similar calculation and using the previous result. Since
\vec{u}
can be chosen freely, it can be chosen so that the left term becomes arbitrarily negative unless
P_{\mu}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}=0.
This concludes the argument that
\rho_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}.

Thus,
\rho_{\lambda}
and
\rho_{\lambda^{\prime}}
have mutually orthogonal supports for
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
provided that
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}.

Hence, although we have no restrictions on
\rho_{\lambda}
when
\lambda\in N_{q},
we still obtain

\rho\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Rlambdastatepreserving})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}q_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}q_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}q_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}P_{\lambda},

(6.11)

which agrees with the result (
5.78
) with respect to a spectral basis, or more accurately (
5.110
), in this special case since
s=1
so that there is only one
i
index and
\sigma_{j}=1
for all
j
because
\sigma_{j}
is a
1\times 1
matrix. Thus, the
\tau_{ji}
matrices reduce to the
\rho_{\lambda}
matrices. To make a more explicit connection to quantum information theory, we recall the definition of a Lüders projection, which is a model for the ensemble of the induced states of a system after a measurement has taken place
[
25
]
.

Definition 6.12
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a density matrix and let
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be self-adjoint with spectrum
\sigma(A).
The
Lüders projection of
\rho
with respect to the measurement of
A
is the density matrix

\rho^{\prime}:=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}.

(6.13)

In summary, we have obtained the following theorem based on our above analysis.

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be a self-adjoint matrix with spectrum
\sigma(A)
, let
F:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}\to\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be as in (
6.1
), and let
\omega={\rm tr}(\rho\;\cdot\;):\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
be a state with
\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle:=\omega\circ F
the induced state on
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}
.
Then
F
has a disintegration of
\omega
over
\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle
consistent with
F
if and only if
\rho
equals its Lüders projection with respect to the measurement of
A
.

Proof.

We will use the same notation as earlier in this section.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume a disintegration exists. By (
6.11
),

P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}=q_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}.
Hence,

\rho_{\lambda}=\frac{P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}}{q_{\lambda}}=\frac{P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}}{{\rm tr}(\rho P_{\lambda})}\qquad\forall\;\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}

(6.15)

because
\rho_{\lambda}
is a density matrix.
Furthermore, since a disintegration exists,

\rho\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:Rlambdastatepreserving})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}q_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:rholambdaisLuders})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}q_{\lambda}\frac{P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}}{q_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}.

(6.16)

(
\Leftarrow
)
Suppose
\rho
equals its Lüders projection, i.e. suppose

\rho=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}.

(6.17)

For each
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
, set
R_{\lambda}:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
to be the linear map defined by

\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\ni B\mapsto R_{\lambda}(B):=\begin{cases}{\rm tr}\left(\frac{P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda}}{q_{\lambda}}B\right)&\mbox{ if }q_{\lambda}>0\\
\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(B)&\mbox{ if }q_{\lambda}=0\\
\end{cases}.

(6.18)

Then the linear map
R:\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}
defined by

R:=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}\mathrm{ev}_{\lambda}^{*}\circ R_{\lambda}
is a disintegration of
F.
To see this, first notice that
R
is positive, which implies it is CP since
{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}
is commutative (cf. Theorem 3 in Stinespring
[
45
]
). Second,
R
is unital because

R(\mathds{1}_{m})=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}R_{\lambda}(\mathds{1}_{m})e_{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda\in N_{q}}R_{\lambda}(\mathds{1}_{m})e_{\lambda}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:disintegrationforLuders})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}e_{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda\in N_{q}}e_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)}e_{\lambda}

(6.19)

since
{\rm tr}(P_{\lambda}\rho P_{\lambda})=q_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in\sigma(A)
.
To show
R
satisfies
R\circ F\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle$}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\sigma(A)}},
we will show
R(F(e_{\mu}))-e_{\mu}\in\mathcal{N}_{\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle}
for all
\mu\in\sigma(A)
. Setting
d_{\mu}:={\rm tr}(P_{\mu}),
the degeneracy/mutliplicity of
\mu\in\sigma(A),
we obtain

\begin{split}R\big(F(e_{\mu})\big)&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:measurementtospectrum})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}R(P_{\mu})=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}R_{\lambda}(P_{\mu})e_{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda\in N_{q}}R_{\lambda}(P_{\mu})e_{\lambda}\\
&\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:disintegrationforLuders})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(A)\setminus N_{q}}\delta_{\mu\lambda}e_{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda\in N_{q}}\frac{d_{\mu}}{m}e_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}e_{\mu}+\frac{d_{\mu}}{m}\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\in N_{q}}e_{\lambda}&\mbox{ if }q_{\mu}>0\\
\frac{d_{\mu}}{m}\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\in N_{q}}e_{\lambda}&\mbox{ if }q_{\mu}=0\\
\end{cases}\end{split}

(6.20)

Hence,
R\big(F(e_{\mu})\big)-e_{\mu}\in{{\mathbb{C}}}^{N_{q}},
which is the null space of
\langle q,\;\cdot\;\rangle
. Thus,
R
is a disintegration.
∎

Appendix A
Equivalent definitions of disintegration

In this appendix, we review the definition of a disintegration
from measure theory (cf. Definition 452E in Fremlin
[
13
]
).
Tables
1
and
2

provide two, a-priori different, definitions of a disintegration with varying input data and consistency conditions.
This appendix serves to explain how these definitions are related to each other.
More precisely,
Theorems
A.12

and
A.23

state that the definitions in the respective tables are equivalent.
Theorem
A.34
says that this diagrammatic
definition of a disintegration is equivalent to the definition
of a regular conditional probability (cf. Definition 2.1 in Panagaden
[
29
]
).

In all that follows,
(X,\Sigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\Omega,\nu)
are measure spaces with no additional assumptions other than
\mu
and
\nu
are non-negative measures. Furthermore,
f:X\to Y
is taken to be measure-preserving so that the pushforward
f_{*}\mu
of
\mu
along
f
is
\nu,
i.e.
\nu(F)=\mu(f^{-1}(F))
for all
F\in\Omega.

Definition A.1
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
and
(Y,\Omega)
be measurable spaces.
A
transition kernel

r
from
(Y,\Omega)
to
(X,\Sigma)
, written
r:Y\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!X,
is a function

r:Y\times\Sigma\to[0,\infty]
such that

i.

r(y,\;\cdot\;):\Sigma\to[0,\infty]
is a measure for all
y\in Y
and

ii.

r(\;\cdot\;,E):Y\to[0,\infty]
is measurable for all
E\in\Sigma.

The notation
r_{y}(E):=r(y,E)
will be implemented. A transition kernel as above is called a
stochastic map
(also
Markov kernel
) when
r_{y}
is a probability measure for all
y\in Y
.

Transition kernels are generalizations of measurable functions in that they assign to each point in the source/domain a measure
on the target/codomain (cf. Example
A.5
). If a function is to be thought of as a deterministic
process, a transition kernel whose associated measures are probability measures can be interpreted as a non-deterministic (i.e. stochastic)
process, where one only knows the probabilities associated with the possible outcomes of that process.

Example A.2
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a measurable space and
let
\{\bullet\}
denote a one element set with the unique
\sigma
-algebra.
There is a bijection between the set of
measures on
(X,\Sigma)
and the set of transition kernels

\{\bullet\}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!X
from
\{\bullet\}
to
X.

This allows measures to be viewed as morphisms.

Definition A.3
.

Let
(X,\Sigma),

(Y,\Omega),
and
(Z,\Xi)
be measurable spaces.
Let
\mu:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Y
and
\nu:Y\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Z
be two transition kernels.
The
composite
of
\mu
followed by
\nu,

written as
\nu\circ\mu:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Z,
is defined by

X\times\Xi\ni(x,E)\mapsto(\nu\circ\mu)_{x}(E):=\int_{Y}\nu_{y}(E)\;d\mu_{x}(y).

(A.4)

This equation is known as the
Chapman–Kolmogorov
equation.

The fact that the composite of transition kernels defines a
transition kernel follows from the monotone convergence theorem
[
42
, Theorem 1.26]
. Rather than proving this here,
we will recall techniques from analysis that can be used to prove this when we prove Theorem
A.12
below.

Example A.5
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
and
(Y,\Omega)
be measurable spaces and let
\mu:\{\bullet\}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!X
be a
measure on
X
and let
f:X\to Y
be a measurable function. Then

f
can be viewed as the transition kernel
f:X\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Y
given by

X\times\Omega\ni(x,E)\mapsto f_{x}(E):=\chi_{E}\big(f(x)\big):=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }f(x)\in E\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(A.6)

Furthermore,
f\circ\mu
is the pushforward
f_{*}\mu
of the measure
\mu
along the map
f
because

\Omega\ni E\mapsto(f\circ\mu)(E)=\int_{X}f_{x}(E)\;d\mu(x)=\int_{X}(\chi_{E}\circ f)\;d\mu=\int_{Y}\chi_{E}\;d(f_{*}\mu)=\mu\big(f^{-1}(E)\big).

(A.7)

A special case of this occurs for the diagonal map

\Delta_{Y}:Y\to Y\times Y
. This pushes forward a
probability measure
q:\{\bullet\}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Y
to the diagonal subset

\Delta_{Y}(Y):=\big\{(y,y)\in Y\times Y\;:\;y\in Y\big\}

(A.8)

of
Y\times Y
so that
(\Delta_{Y}\circ q)(A\times B)=q(A\cap B)
for all
A,B\in\Omega
.
This map is used to instantiate a categorical formulation of a.e. equivalence (cf. Remark
A.21
).

Example A.9
.

Let
X,Y,
and
Z
be finite sets equipped with the discrete
\sigma
-algebra
and suppose that all transition kernels are stochastic maps.
Then Definitions
A.1

and
A.3

reproduce the notion of
stochastic matrices
including their compositions. Indeed, since the
\sigma
-algebra on
Y
is discrete,

f_{x}(E)=\sum_{y\in E}f_{x}(\{y\})

(A.10)

so that the probability measure
f_{x}
is determined by its values
on points of
Y.
We therefore write
f_{yx}:=f_{x}(\{y\})
to denote the

yx
entry of
f
in matrix form. Second, the composite
X\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

sends
x\in X
to the probability
measure on
Z
determined by

\begin{split}Z\ni z\xmapsto{(g\circ f)(x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}:=\sum_{y\in Y}g_{zy}f_{yx}.\end{split}

(A.11)

Following Example
A.5
, when
Y
is a finite set equipped with the discrete
\sigma
-algebra, the pushforward of
q:\{\bullet\}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!Y
along
\Delta_{Y}:Y\to Y\times Y
simplifies to
(\Delta_{Y}\circ q)_{(y,y^{\prime})}=\delta_{yy^{\prime}}q_{y}.

Conditions

\int_{X}h\;d\mu=\int_{Y}\left(\int_{X}h\;dr_{y}\right)d\nu(y)

\forall
measurable
h:X\to[0,\infty]

\mu(E)=\int_{Y}r_{y}(E)\;d\nu(y)

\forall\;E\in\Sigma

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{r}

i.e.
r\circ\nu=\mu
